# Chapter XXX.

### Whether in the approaching golden age there shall be an earthly kingdom and state: also whether one shall make use of worldly glory, fleshly pleasures, and joys in the same.

In this world, it happens that blind, worldly men always look only upon the fleshly, earthly, and transitory; and commonly place their highest welfare and hope in the same; they also hardly believe in their hearts that there should be another kind of more glorious and better life than what one can have in this world in the form of wealth, honor, splendor, and all kinds of pleasures. Indeed, even the most devout, careful, and clever among the children of the world commonly concern themselves most intensely with how, in this current and so dangerous state of the world, it will finally turn out regarding their earthly and transitory <term>possessions</term> <note>original: *possessionen*</note>, goods, and bodily freedom. Whether the Turk <note>a common 17th-century term for the Ottoman Empire, which was then viewed as a significant military threat to Europe</note> shall yet conquer these lands; or whether we, on the contrary, shall overcome him and enrich ourselves from his land and people.

<summary>This page concludes a description of a Ptolemaic astronomical clock and begins a theological inquiry into whether the promised 'Golden Age' will be a physical kingdom of earthly delights or a spiritual state.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical instruments, Ptolemaic sphere, clockwork, Golden Age, eschatology, Ottoman Empire, materialism</keywords>

On this matter, whenever philosophers <note>original: *Weltweise*, literally "world-wise ones"</note> or even common folk gather, all their talk and counsel, their imaginings and strivings, their prophesying and prognosticating <note>original: *prognosticiren*</note> is focused on these earthly things. One does not easily hear anyone mention how things might generally turn out—for Turks <note>a reference to the Ottoman Empire</note> as well as Christians, for Lutherans as well as Papists <note>a common, often polemical term for Roman Catholics in this period</note>, and thus for one just as for the other—when this entire world comes to an end and all things are transformed. Indeed, anyone who sought to bring such matters into discussion among "world-wise" people would soon be laughed at or else dismissed as a fool.

Such people were also the Jews at the close of the first <term>century</term> <note>original: *Seculi*</note>, and they remain so to this day. For everything that the prophets of the Old and New Testaments prophesied regarding the final coming of the <term>Messiah</term> <note>original: *Messiae*</note>, they understand solely in terms of fleshly and earthly things. They believe that such a <term>Messiah</term> shall come who, according to the nature and character of this current world, will be a great and powerful king and <term>monarch</term> <note>original: *Monarcha*</note>. They believe he will deliver them alone from their physical servitude, shame, and contempt—in which they live among Christians, Turks, and other nations—and make them into great and powerful people in this present world.

In the beginning of this current <term>century</term> <note>original: *Seculi*</note>, this great <term>error</term> <note>original: *Irrthumb*</note> had also, according to human weakness, so possessed the disciples of the Lord Christ that they (until they were enlightened by the <term>Holy Spirit</term> <note>original: *Heiliger Geist*</note>...

(who both were <term>disciples</term><note>original: *Discipuli*</note> of the Apostle John) and many other <term>Church Fathers</term><note>original: *Patres*</note> after them—whom we mentioned above in Chapter 6—well knew and understood. These two aforementioned bishops would not have defended these things so stubbornly if they had not perhaps heard them from the mouth of John himself, and the others from them in turn. Accordingly, it would have been proper for some of those who came after them to judge the opinions of these people more modestly, and to interpret these things in the best possible light. We made mention of this above in Chapter 6.

Therefore, no one will persuade us that it ever entered the minds or thoughts of these holy and godly Christian ancestors that Christ should ever establish an earthly kingdom in this mortal, transitory life and in this vile, godless world, in which one would indulge in carnal, sinful pleasures and desires. "...for those same thousand years they shall generate an infinite multitude, and their offspring shall be holy and dear to God." <note>original Latin: *eosdem mille annos infinitatem multitudinem generabunt, & erit soboles eorum sancta, & Deo chara.* This quote is likely from Lactantius, whom the author was discussing on the previous page.</note> Others may interpret and understand such words as they wish: but for our part—in honor of this excellent man and to interpret him in the best possible light—we would not have them understood as if he were speaking in that place of <term>carnal procreation</term> <note>original: *Fleischlicher Kinderzeugung*</note> which occurs through physical intercourse. ## Conclusion.

What I have written in the present <term>treatise</term> <note>original: "Tractat"</note> are almost only the *rinds* <note>original: "Cortices," meaning the outer husks or shells</note> or hulls of the noble fruits of the highest study of Divine Wisdom. Nevertheless, in these the kindhearted, impartial reader—if he takes diligent heed—will undoubtedly notice a considerable difference from other similar writings published until now, owing to several verses from the Word of God explained therein *harmoniously* <note>original: "Harmonicè," referring to the mystical alignment or internal consistency of scripture</note> according to the secret understanding. Therefore, the reader can all the more reasonably infer for himself what great excellence and preciousness the core of the aforementioned fruits must contain in itself. This is especially true regarding the actual nature and occasion of the approaching final <term>golden age</term> <note>a reference to the "Golden Age" or "Sabbath of Time," a period of peace and divine rule expected by many 17th-century mystics</note> *in particular*:

Concerning this—as far as such things have been *revealed* <note>original: "revelirt"</note> and entrusted to me by the Spirit of God—I have written a special secret book: which among other things, but especially concerning that same golden age, is a true *key* <note>original: "Clavis"</note> to the Prophetic and Apostolic writings; by which the great treasures and mysteries of God buried and hidden within them are, in considerable measure, unlocked and opened. For when you look even at the New Testament, there are indeed many new things in it which were previously quite unknown to the Jews, yet which nonetheless were heartily and stately (yet *mystically* <note>original: "mysticè tamen"</note>) grounded in the Prophetic writings of the Old Testament; these are first explained by the

# Theological Epistles

->Regarding the<-

# Right Way of Going to Church /

->To Magister <note>A "Magister" was a high-level academic degree, similar to a Master of Arts, common among clergy at the time.</note> G. S., a Court Preacher.<-

In which is briefly demonstrated and proven, from the true foundation and understanding of the Holy Scripture, as well as according to the testimonies of the most eminent ancient and modern theologians: how far, and to what extent not, going to church is useful and necessary for a person seeking God. Alongside this is a letter, in which may be seen what true nobility <note>The author explores "spiritual nobility" versus inherited social rank.</note> is.

->Written several years ago by<-

<insert>Abraham <gloss>Abrak</gloss> von Franckenberg</insert>

A man enlightened by God, also very experienced in languages and mysteries, a nobleman from Silesia <note>A historical region in Central Europe, now mostly in Poland.</note>. But now, after his death, published <note>original: "zum Druck befordert," literally "promoted to print."</note> by a lover of the truth.

->In Amsterdam, they are to be obtained in Eland Street, at the sign of the Salamander, 1667.<-

<insert>J. D. Pennert.</insert>

<summary>This is the title page of a 1667 collection of theological letters by the Silesian mystic Abraham von Franckenberg, addressing the necessity of church attendance and the nature of true nobility.</summary>
